## Reasoning

**Understanding the mission:**
- Before switch time: score += 1 per moving robot → robots should be **stationary** (Stop)
- After switch time: score += 1 per moving robot → wait, re-reading...
- Before switch: score increases by 1 for every **stationary** robot → robots should **move**
- After switch: score increases by 1 for every **moving** robot → robots should **stop**

Wait, let me re-read:
- Before switch: +1 per **stationary** robot → minimize by **moving**
- After switch: +1 per **moving** robot → minimize by **stopping**

The green light signals the switch. So:
- Phase 1 (before green light): robots should **MOVE** (exploration/random walk)
- Phase 2 (after green light appears): robots should **STOP**

The key sensor: **C=7 (perceives green color, l=2)** triggers the switch from moving to stopping.

The champion (score 7777.62) has states with GoToColor(green), Attraction, Stop(x2). It seems partially working. The ideal design:
- State 0: Exploration (moving) - before switch
- State 1: Stop - after seeing green light
- Transition: C=7 with l=2 (green) → switch from exploration to stop

Let me design machines around this core idea:

**Core FSM (2-state ideal):**
- S0: Exploration (B=0), transition on C=7 (green seen, high prob) → S1
- S1: Stop (B=1), possibly transition back if no green (low prob)

**Champion:** Keep verbatim.

**Explorations:** Try different topologies - 2-state, 3-state with clear green-detection logic.

## Reasoning

The new champion (score 7414.76) has:
- S0: GoAwayColor(green), transitions: on seeing green (C=7, p=0.85) → S1 (Stop), or random (C=5, p=0.003) → S1 (Stop)
- S1: Stop, transition: random (C=5, p=0.004) → S1 (self-loop? No, destination=1 but that's index among others...)

Wait, let me re-read. With 3 states (0,1,2), leaving state 1 the others are [0,2]. So `--n1x0 1` means destination index 1 among [0,2] = state 2.

So:
- S0: GoAwayColor(green) → on green seen → S1(Stop) or random tiny prob → S1(Stop)
- S1: Stop → random (p=0.004) → S2 (Exploration)
- S2: Exploration (rwm=40) → on green seen → S1 (Stop)

This makes sense! Before green: robots are in S0 (moving away from green - but wait, GoAwayColor needs to see green to move away from it, and before the switch there's no green wall). So before the switch, GoAwayColor probably just wanders randomly (no green to avoid), then after green appears it transitions to Stop. Then occasionally goes to explore, then back to stop when green seen again.

Actually the logic is: S0 = GoAwayColor (moves randomly when no green visible, moves away when green visible). The transition on C=7 (green seen) with p=0.85 goes to S1 (Stop). So when green appears, high chance of stopping. S1 occasionally transitions to S2 (explore), S2 goes back to S1 on green. This is elegant!

But wait - before switch time, robots should MOVE (to avoid stationary penalty). After switch they should STOP. In S0 (GoAwayColor), robots are moving (good for phase 1). When green appears, they stop (good for phase 2). The occasional S1→S2→S1 cycling keeps some motion in phase 1 if they accidentally transition.

**Key insight:** The GoAwayColor in S0 keeps robots moving before the green light. When green appears, they stop. This is the right behavior.

**Improvements to try:**
1. Make the S0→S1 transition probability higher (e.g., 0.95) for faster response to green
2. Try Exploration instead of GoAwayColor in S0 (might explore more efficiently)
3. Reduce the random escape probability from Stop (0.004 is already low)
4. Try 2-state: Explore → Stop on green
5. Add more transitions to S0 to keep moving (neighbor conditions)

Let me design 14 machines:

1. **Champion (verbatim)**
2. **Exploit: higher green-detection prob (0.95)**
3. **Exploit: lower random escape from Stop (0.002)**
4. **Exploit: Exploration instead of GoAwayColor in S0**
5. **Exploit: different rwm in S2**
6. **Exploit: S1 has two transitions**
7. **Explore: 2-state Explore→Stop**
8. **Explore: 4-state with GoToColor detour**
9. **Explore: 3-state with Attraction in S2**
10. **Explore: 2-state GoAwayColor→Stop**
11. **Explore: 3-state all different**
12. **Exploit: tweak p0x0 to 0.9**
13. **Exploit: tweak p0x0 to 0.95, p1x0 lower**
14. **Explore: 3-state with repulsion**

Note on S1 self-loop issue: In the champion, `--n1x0 1` with 3 states leaving state 1 means destination=1 among [0,2] = state 2. So S1→S2. And S2→S1. That's correct.

## Reasoning

The new champion (score 5719.71) has 4 states:
- S0: GoAwayColor(green) → on green seen (p=0.85) → S1(Stop), or random (p=0.002) → S1(Stop)
- S1: Stop → random (p=0.004) → S2(Explore), or green seen (p=0.8) → S3(Stop)
- S2: Explore(rwm=40) → green seen (p=0.95) → S1(Stop), or random (p=0.002) → S3(Stop)
- S3: Stop → random (p=0.003) → S1(Stop)

Destination indexing check:
- From S0: others=[1,2,3], n0x0=1→S1 ✓, n0x1=1→S1 ✓
- From S1: others=[0,2,3], n1x0=1→S2 ✓, n1x1=2→S3 ✓
- From S2: others=[0,1,3], n2x0=1→S1 ✓, n2x1=2→S3 ✓
- From S3: others=[0,1,2], n3x0=1→S1 ✓

This is elegant: S0 (moving before green), green triggers Stop. S1/S3 are both stop states with reinforcing green detection. S2 is a brief exploration that quickly returns to stop when green seen.

**Key parameters to exploit:**
- S0 green prob: 0.85 (try 0.88, 0.9, 0.92)
- S1 green prob: 0.8 (try 0.85, 0.75)
- S2 green prob: 0.95 (already high, try 0.98)
- S2 random escape: 0.002 (try 0.001)
- S3 random escape: 0.003 (try 0.002, 0.004)
- rwm in S2: 40 (try 20, 60)

**Explorations:**
- Add green detection to S3 (reinforce stop)
- Try S0 with Exploration instead of GoAwayColor
- 3-state simplified version
- Add neighbor condition to S0 (help spreading)

## Reasoning

The champion (score 5710.02) is very similar to the previous one but with S0 green prob bumped to 0.9 (from 0.85). The topology is stable:
- S0: GoAwayColor(green) → Stop(S1) on green (p=0.9) or random (p=0.002)
- S1: Stop → Explore(S2) random (p=0.004), or Stop(S3) on green (p=0.8)
- S2: Explore(rwm=40) → Stop(S1) on green (p=0.95), or Stop(S3) random (p=0.002)
- S3: Stop → Stop(S1) random (p=0.003)

The improvement from 5719→5710 is tiny (0.17%), suggesting we're near a local optimum in this topology. We need to both exploit finely and explore new topologies.

**Key observations:**
- The dual-stop structure (S1+S3) works well
- Green detection drives stopping efficiently
- S2 (explore) acts as a recovery state when robots accidentally stop before switch
- The random escapes from stop states allow some movement before switch

**Fine exploitation ideas:**
- S0 green prob: try 0.92, 0.95
- S1 green prob: try 0.85, 0.9
- S2 green prob: try 0.97, 0.98
- S1 random escape: try 0.003, 0.005 (more movement before switch)
- S3 random escape: try 0.002, 0.004
- rwm in S2: try 15, 30, 50

**Structural explorations:**
- Add green detection to S3 (reinforce it as stop state)
- Try S0 with Attraction (keeps robots moving and clustered)
- Try 3-state simplified
- Add S3→S2 transition to allow more exploration before switch

# ROUND 7/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion hasn't improved in round 6, staying at 5710.02. We're stuck at a local optimum with this topology. I need to think more carefully about what's limiting performance.

**Analysis of the scoring:**
- Phase 1 (before green, ~400-600 steps): robots should MOVE → score += stationary robots
- Phase 2 (after green, ~600-800 steps): robots should STOP → score += moving robots

**Lower bound estimate:** If all 20 robots move perfectly in phase 1 and stop perfectly in phase 2:
- Phase 1: 0 penalty (all moving)
- Phase 2: 0 penalty (all stopped)
- Total: 0

But 5710 is quite high. With 1200 steps total and ~500 steps phase 1, ~700 steps phase 2:
- If robots stop immediately at switch: 20×700 = 14000 (all stopped, 0 penalty after switch)
- Wait, moving robots penalized after switch, stopped robots penalized before switch
- Perfect: move for ~500 steps (0 penalty), stop for ~700 steps (0 penalty) = 0 total

So 5710 suggests significant time spent in wrong state. The current champion:
- S0: GoAwayColor (moving) - good for phase 1
- But if robots accidentally enter S1/S3 (Stop) during phase 1, they accrue penalty
- The S1 random escape (p=0.004) is the main path back to moving

**Key insight I may have missed:** The random escape probability from Stop is very low (0.004/step). If a robot enters Stop during phase 1, it stays stopped for ~250 steps on average before escaping. This is very costly!

**Better strategy:** Make the stop states more "conditional" - only stay stopped when green is visible. If green is NOT visible (phase 1), escape quickly. But we can't directly detect "no green" - only "sees green" (C=7).

Actually C=7 with low p can serve as a "not green" proxy... but that's backwards.

**Alternative approach:** Make escape from Stop probabilistically faster during phase 1 (before green appears). But robots can't know what phase they're in unless they see green.

**Better design:** 
- In Stop state, transition back to moving state based on NOT seeing green (use low p on C=7 as filter, or use C=5 with higher p)
- Higher random escape = more movement in phase 1 but also more penalty in phase 2

The tension: higher escape prob → better phase 1 (more moving) but worse phase 2 (less stopping).

**Optimal escape prob calculation:** 
- Phase 1 lasts ~500 steps. If p_escape=0.004, expected time in Stop = 250 steps, so a robot that enters Stop midway through phase 1 loses ~125 steps.
- Phase 2 lasts ~700 steps. With p_escape=0.004, robot re-enters moving state on average once every 250 steps, spending ~1/250 fraction of time moving. With 700 steps: 700×0.004 ≈ 2.8 escapes, each lasting a few steps.

The real problem: robots initially in S0 (GoAwayColor) should stay moving. S0 only transitions to Stop when green seen or with p=0.002. So in phase 1, robots mostly stay in S0 (moving) - good! The penalty comes from the ~0.002/step random transition to Stop.

Expected random Stop entries in phase 1 (500 steps): 500 × 0.002 = 1 per robot. Each costs ~250 steps in Stop = 250 × 20 = 5000 total... that's too much!

Actually if p_escape=0.004, expected time stopped = 1/0.004 = 250 steps. So each accidental stop costs 250 steps × 20 robots × 1 stop = 5000 step-penalties. That's basically our score!

**Fix:** Either make the S0→Stop random prob even lower (0.001 or 0.0005), OR make the escape from Stop much faster.

But faster escape = worse phase 2. The real fix: make Stop escape depend on whether green is visible. If green NOT visible → escape fast. If green visible → stay stopped.

We can't directly do "not green" but we can have:
- Stop state: fast random escape (p=0.01) back to moving
- Stop state: if green seen → go to "permanent stop" state with very slow escape

This way: during phase 1, robots quickly escape Stop; during phase 2, green detection locks them in permanent stop.

**New topology:**
- S0: GoAwayColor(green) → S1(Stop_temp) on green (p=0.9), random (p=0.001)
- S1: Stop (fast escape, p=0.01) → S0 (move), or if green seen → S2 (Stop_perm)  
- S2: Stop_permanent → very slow escape (p=0.001) → S1

Let me design several variants of this idea plus exploitations of champion.

Destination indexing for 4 states:
- From S0: others=[1,2,3], 0→S1, 1→S2, 2→S3
- From S1: others=[0,2,3], 0→S0, 1→S2, 2→S3
- From S2: others=[0,1,3], 0→S0, 1→S1, 2→S3
- From S3: others=[0,1,2], 0→S0, 1→S1, 2→S2

For 3 states:
- From S0: others=[1,2], 0→S1, 1→S2
- From S1: others=[0,2], 0→S0, 1→S2
- From S2: others=[0,1], 0→S0, 1→S1

FSM: --nstates 4 --s0 9 --vel0 1.0 --cle0 0 --clr0 2 --n0 2 --n0x0 1 --c0x0 7 --p0x0 0.9 --l0x0 2 --n0x1 1 --c0x1 5 --p0x1 0.002 --s1 1 --cle1 5 --n1 2 --n1x0 1 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.004 --n1x1 2 --c1x1 7 --p1x1 0.8 --l1x1 2 --s2 0 --rwm2 40 --cle2 0 --n2 2 --n2x0 1 --c2x0 7 --p2x0 0.95 --l2x0 2 --n2x1 2 --c2x1 5 --p2x1 0.002 --s3 1 --cle3 0 --n3 1 --n3x0 1 --c3x0 5 --p3x0 0.003

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion improved slightly to 5629.04 (from 5710.02), and it's the same topology as before but with a minor parameter tweak found. We're still stuck near this local optimum.

**Deep analysis of the problem:**

The champion structure:
- S0: GoAwayColor(green) [moving] → S1(Stop) on green (p=0.9) or random (p=0.002)
- S1: Stop → S2(Explore) random (p=0.004), or → S3(Stop) on green (p=0.8)
- S2: Explore(rwm=40) → S1(Stop) on green (p=0.95), or → S3(Stop) random (p=0.002)
- S3: Stop → S1(Stop) random (p=0.003)

The key bottleneck I identified: robots in S1/S3 during phase 1 incur penalty. The escape rate is p=0.004/step, meaning ~250 steps to escape. With random entry at p=0.002/step from S0, in 500 steps we expect ~1 accidental Stop entry per robot.

**New insight:** The new champion in round 7 is the SAME as round 5/6 champion but got a slightly better score (5629 vs 5710) - likely due to simulation variance. The topology is solid but needs a fundamentally different escape mechanism.

**Key new strategy:** Make S1 (the temporary stop) escape back to S0 quickly (high p), while only S3 is the "permanent stop" locked by green. This way:
- Phase 1: accidentally enter S1 → escape quickly (high p=0.02-0.05) back to S0 (moving)
- Phase 2: green detected → S3 (permanent stop, very low escape p=0.001)

**Revised topology:**
- S0: GoAwayColor → S3(perm-stop) on green (high p), or → S1(temp-stop) random (tiny p)
- S1: Stop [temp] → S0 (quick escape, p=0.03) 
- S2: Explore → S3(perm-stop) on green (high p), or → S1(temp-stop) random (tiny p)  
- S3: Stop [perm] → S2(explore) very rarely (p=0.001)

Wait, but S3 needs to be a true permanent stop. Let me reconsider destinations.

For 4 states, from S0: others=[1,2,3]. Let me map:
- S0: GoAwayColor → on green → S3 (n0x0=2→S3), random → S1 (n0x1=0→S1)
- S1: Stop[temp] → escape to S0 (n1x0=0→S0) fast (p=0.03), or → S3 on green (n1x1=2→S3)
- S2: Explore → on green → S3 (n2x0=2→S3), random → S1 (n2x1=0→S0? no, 1→S1)
- S3: Stop[perm] → S2 very rarely (n3x0=2→S2, p=0.001)

Actually let me think about what "others" means from each state:
- From S0 (state 0): others=[1,2,3], index 0=S1, 1=S2, 2=S3
- From S1 (state 1): others=[0,2,3], index 0=S0, 1=S2, 2=S3
- From S2 (state 2): others=[0,1,3], index 0=S0, 1=S1, 2=S3
- From S3 (state 3): others=[0,1,2], index 0=S0, 1=S1, 2=S2

**New design A (fast-escape S1, permanent S3):**
- S0: GoAwayColor → green(p=0.9)→S3, random(p=0.001)→S1
- S1: Stop[temp] → fast escape(p=0.02)→S0, green(p=0.85)→S3
- S2: Explore(rwm=40) → green(p=0.95)→S3, random(p=0.001)→S1
- S3: Stop[perm] → very rare(p=0.001)→S2

**New design B (2-state: move/stop with fast escape):**
- S0: GoAwayColor → green(p=0.9)→S1
- S1: Stop → green(p=0.5, reinforcement) stay?, random(p=0.005)→S0

**New design C (exploit champion with tiny tweaks):**
Various parameter variations around the champion.

Let me also consider: maybe the score 5629 is actually close to optimal for this approach. The theoretical minimum with perfect behavior:
- Phase 1 (~500 steps): 20 robots moving = 0 penalty
- Phase 2 (~700 steps): 20 robots stopped = 0 penalty  
- Total: 0

But robots can't instantly stop at switch. With green detection probability p=0.9/step, expected detection delay = 1/0.9 ≈ 1.1 steps. So ~1 step × 20 robots = 20 penalty at switch. That's tiny.

The real penalty comes from: (1) phase 1 accidental stops, (2) phase 2 accidental moves.

With 5629 score over 1200 steps, 20 robots: average ~0.23 penalty per robot per step. That means ~23% of robot-steps are in wrong state. That's still quite high.

Let me try the fast-escape approach more aggressively:

# ROUND 9/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

We're stuck at 5629.04 for two rounds. The fast-escape approach (S1→S0 with p=0.015-0.025) didn't beat the champion. Let me think more carefully.

**Re-examining the problem:**

The champion has S1 (Stop) escaping to S2 (Explore) with p=0.004, meaning robots stay stopped ~250 steps on average. During phase 1 (~500 steps), if a robot accidentally enters S1, it's stuck for ~250 steps - huge penalty.

But the fast-escape variants I tried (p=0.015-0.025 back to S0) didn't improve things. Why?

**New insight:** Maybe the issue is that in phase 2, after robots stop, they need to STAY stopped. With fast escape (p=0.02), expected stop duration = 50 steps, then they explore again for a bit before detecting green and stopping again. With 700 steps of phase 2: ~14 escapes × a few steps each = maybe 50 steps moving = 50×20=1000 penalty. That's worse than 0.004 escape (which gives 700×0.004×a_few_steps ≈ much less).

**Actually the bottleneck may be different:** The robots start in S0 (GoAwayColor). GoAwayColor without any visible green light = the robot still moves (it moves randomly when no target is visible). So in phase 1, ALL robots are moving (good!). They only enter Stop when green is seen (p=0.9 chance) or random (p=0.002).

Wait - GoAwayColor: does it move when no green is visible? The behavior description says "moves away from the nearest object of a target colour" - if no such object is visible, it may just move forward/randomly. So S0 keeps robots moving before switch.

After switch (green appears), p=0.9 per step to go to S1 (Stop). Expected delay = 1/0.9 ≈ 1.1 steps. Very fast! So phase 2 detection is nearly instant. Then S3 has p=0.003 escape, meaning ~333 steps stay stopped. Phase 2 lasts ~700 steps, so maybe 2 escapes × a few explore steps each. That's the residual penalty.

The score of 5629 with 20 robots over 1200 steps: 5629/(20×1200) ≈ 0.234 penalty per robot-step. That's 23% of time in wrong state.

**Where does 23% come from?**
- Phase 1 (~500 steps): robots mostly in S0 (moving) = good. But S1/S3 are Stop = bad. If random entry p=0.002/step, expected entries = 1 per robot per 500 steps. Each stay = ~250 steps. So 250/500 = 50% of phase 1 in Stop?? That seems too high.

Wait, but once in S1, there's also a green-detection escape. Before switch, no green visible, so C=7 never fires. So indeed only p=0.004 escape from S1, and p=0.8 green→S3 which also won't fire. So escape is purely p=0.004.

Actually with p=0.002 entry per step and p=0.004 escape per step, in steady state: fraction stopped = 0.002/(0.002+0.004) = 33%. That's very high!

**The fix:** Either reduce entry probability (already 0.002) or increase escape probability. But increasing escape worsens phase 2.

**Better approach:** Make S0 have NO random transition to Stop. Only transition to Stop when green is seen. The p=0.002 random transition is the culprit!

If we remove the random transition from S0, robots stay in S0 (moving) throughout phase 1, then instantly stop when green appears. Phase 2 penalty = only escape events from Stop.

Let me try: S0 with only green detection → Stop. No random escape.

But then robots might get stuck in S0 forever if green never appears (before switch). That's actually GOOD - we want them moving in phase 1!

**New champion-beating design:**
- S0: GoAwayColor(green) → S1(Stop) ONLY on green seen (p=0.95), NO random transition
- S1: Stop → S2(Explore) random (p=0.004), or → S3(Stop) on green (p=0.85)
- S2: Explore → S3(Stop) on green (p=0.95), NO random transition back to Stop
- S3: Stop → very rare escape (p=0.002) → S0 (to keep moving if somehow in wrong state)

This way phase 1: robots stay in S0 (moving), never accidentally stop!
Phase 2: green → S1/S3, escape rare.

Let me implement this. From S0 with 1 transition: n0=1, n0x0=0→S1(index 0 among [1,2,3]=S1).

Actually I realize - does GoAwayColor with no visible green just move forward? If so, it's fine. If it stops, then we need Exploration in S0.

Let me try both GoAwayColor and Exploration for S0.

Also try: what if S2 (Explore) has NO random exit, only green detection? Then phase 1 robots in S2 stay exploring (moving) until green appears.

**Design variants to try:**

A: S0=GoAwayColor, 1 transition (green only) → S1; S1=Stop, 2 transitions (random→S2, green→S3); S2=Explore, 1 transition (green→S3); S3=Stop, 1 transition (random→S1)

B: S0=Explore, 1 transition (green only) → S1; same rest

C: Keep champion but reduce random transition from S0 to 0.001

D: Champion topology but with S0 having only green transition (no random)

E: Add a "recovery" state that's Explore, entered from Stop only rarely

## Reasoning

The new champion (5513.35) improved by tweaking probabilities: S0 green prob = 0.93, S1 green prob = 0.82, S2 green prob = 0.96. The topology is unchanged. This is the final round, so I need to make it count.

**Analysis of current champion:**
- S0: GoAwayColor(green) → Stop(S1) on green (p=0.93), random (p=0.002) → S1
- S1: Stop → Explore(S2) random (p=0.004), or → Stop(S3) on green (p=0.82)
- S2: Explore(rwm=40) → Stop(S1) on green (p=0.96), or → Stop(S3) random (p=0.002)
- S3: Stop → Stop(S1) random (p=0.003)

**Parameter sensitivity analysis:**
- S0 green prob: 0.93 (try 0.95, 0.97, 0.92)
- S1 green prob: 0.82 (try 0.85, 0.88, 0.8)
- S2 green prob: 0.96 (try 0.97, 0.98, 0.95)
- S1 random escape: 0.004 (try 0.003, 0.005)
- S2 random escape: 0.002 (try 0.001, 0.003)
- S3 random escape: 0.003 (try 0.002, 0.004)
- rwm in S2: 40 (try 20, 30, 50, 60)

**Key insight from no-random-transition variants:** The machines with only green detection on S0 (no random escape) didn't beat champion, but maybe combined with the right green prob values they could.

**Strategy for final round:**
1. Champion verbatim (elitism)
2. Fine-tune champion: increase S0 green prob (0.95, 0.97)
3. Fine-tune: increase S1 green prob (0.85, 0.88)
4. Fine-tune: increase S2 green prob (0.97, 0.98)
5. Fine-tune: reduce all random escapes
6. Combined: higher green probs + lower random
7. Try no-random-from-S0 with updated probs
8. Try rwm variations
9. Try S3 with green detection added

For the final push, I'll focus on exploiting the proven topology with careful parameter tuning, plus a few structural variants.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
